    Headline
    bone fractures, osteopenia
    General Information
    In the CANVAS trial, bone fractures were reported with an incidence rate of 1.09, 1.59, and 1.79 per 100 patient-years of follow-up for the placebo, canagliflozin 100 mg/day, and canagliflozin 300 mg/day groups, respectively. Fractures occurred within the first 26 weeks of therapy and remained through the end of the trial. Fractures were more likely to be low trauma (e.g., fall from not more than standing height) and affect the distal portion of upper and lower extremities. In a pool analysis of 9 clinical studies, falls occurred in 1.3%, 1.5%, and 2.1% of patients receiving comparator, canagliflozin 100 mg/day, and canagliflozin 300 mg/day, respectively. The increased risk of falls was observed in patients receiving canagliflozin in the first few weeks of treatment. There may be a risk for osteopenia. In a clinical trial of 714 older patients (age 55 to 80 years), patients randomized to canagliflozin 100 mg and canagliflozin 300 mg had placebo corrected declines in bone mineral density (BMD) at the total hip of 0.9% and 1.2%, respectively, and at the lumbar spine of 0.3% and 0.7%, respectively, at 2 years. Additionally, placebo adjusted BMD declines were 0.1% at the femoral neck for both canagliflozin doses and 0.4% at the distal forearm for patients randomized to canagliflozin 300 mg. Consider factors that contribute to fracture risk before initiating canagliflozin; metformin. Advise patients to discuss any risk factors for bone fractures with their healthcare professional but not to discontinue or change their diabetes medications without first talking to their healthcare professional.

    Headline
    necrotizing fasciitis, tissue necrosis
    General Information
    Canagliflozin and other sodium-glucose co-transporter 2 (SGLT2) inhibitors increase urinary glucose excretion and increase the risk of genitourinary infections including urinary tract infections and genital mycotic infections in both male and female subjects. Serious genitourinary infections, including urosepsis, pyelonephritis, and a rare, and life-threatening infection called necrotizing fasciitis (tissue necrosis) of the perineum, also referred to as Fournier's gangrene have been reported. Some cases have resulted in hospitalization, multiple surgical procedures, or death. Counsel individuals to promptly seek medical attention if they experience any symptoms of tenderness, erythema, or swelling in the genital or perineal area, fever, or malaise. If Fournier's gangrene is suspected, discontinue canagliflozin; metformin and institute prompt treatment with antibiotics and surgical debridement, if appropriate. Closely monitor blood glucose levels, and provide appropriate alternative therapy for glycemic control.

      GSTerm Name
      tissue necrosis
      Severity
      Severe
      GSTerm Name
      necrotizing fasciitis
      Severity
      Severe
    Headline
    lower limb amputation, skin ulcer
    General Information
    Infection and lower limb amputation have been reported in association with some SGLT2 inhibitors, including canagliflozin. Results from the CANVAS and CANVAS-R studies have shown an increase in leg and foot amputations with canagliflozin in subjects who had either established cardiovascular disease or who were at risk for cardiovascular disease. Amputations were mostly involving the toe and midfoot (99 out of 140 subjects in both trials); amputations involving the leg, below and above the knee, were also observed (41 out of 140 subjects in both trials). The risk of lower limb amputations over 1 year's time in the CANVAS study was 5.9 amputations per 1,000 subjects treated with canagliflozin compared to 2.8 amputations per 1,000 subjects treated with placebo. In the CANVAS-R study, the risk of lower limb amputations over 1 year's time was 7.5 amputations per 1,000 subjects for canagliflozin compared to 4.2 amputations per 1,000 subjects for placebo. Some subjects had more than 1 amputation, some involving both limbs. In the CREDENCE trial, the rate of lower limb amputations associated with the use of canagliflozin 100 mg relative to placebo was 12.3 vs. 11.2 events per 1,000 person-years, respectively. Counsel recipients of canagliflozin; metformin about the importance of routine preventative foot care. Monitor individuals for infection (osteomyelitis) of the legs and feet and instruct individuals to notify their healthcare professional immediately if they notice a new sore or skin ulcer, any new pain or tenderness, unusual sensations, skin color changes, or infection in their legs or feet, and institute appropriate treatment. The individual should self-examine their feet daily during routine preventative diabetes care, and a healthcare professional should routinely perform a complete foot exam.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms

    Headline
    diabetic ketoacidosis
    General Information
    Reports of ketoacidosis, a serious, life-threatening condition requiring urgent hospitalization have been identified in clinical trials and postmarketing surveillance in patients with type 1 and type 2 diabetes mellitus receiving sodium glucose co-transporter-2 (SGLT2) inhibitors, including canagliflozin. Fatal cases of ketoacidosis have been reported in patients taking canagliflozin. The risk of ketoacidosis may be greater with higher doses. Canagliflozin significantly increases the risk of diabetic ketoacidosis in patients with type 1 diabetes mellitus. In many of the postmarketing reports, and particularly in patients with type 1 diabetes, the presence of ketoacidosis was not immediately recognized, and the institution of treatment was delayed because presenting blood glucose levels were below those typically expected for DKA (often less than 250 mg/dL). Signs and symptoms at presentation were consistent with dehydration and severe metabolic acidosis and included nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain, generalized malaise, and shortness of breath. In some but not all cases, factors predisposing to ketoacidosis such as acute febrile illness, infection, reduced caloric intake, ketogenic diet, surgery, reduction in dose of exogenous insulin or discontinuation of exogenous insulin or insulin secretagogue, volume depletion, or alcohol abuse were identified. Before initiating canagliflozin; metformin, consider factors in the patient history that may predispose to ketoacidosis including pancreatic insulin deficiency, caloric restriction, volume depletion, and alcohol abuse. Risk factors for ketoacidosis also include patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus and pancreatic disorders such as, a history of pancreatitis, pancreatic insufficiency, or pancreatic surgery. Ketoacidosis and glucosuria may persist longer than typically expected. Urinary glucose excretion persists for 3 days after discontinuing canagliflozin; metformin; however there have been postmarketing reports of ketoacidosis and glucosuria lasting greater than 6 days and some up to 2 weeks after discontinuation of SGLT2 inhibitors. Based on the clinical situation, consider ketone monitoring in persons with type 1 diabetes mellitus and in others at risk for ketoacidosis. Patients treated with canagliflozin; metformin who present with signs and symptoms consistent with dehydration and severe metabolic acidosis should be assessed for ketoacidosis regardless of presenting blood glucose levels, as ketoacidosis may be present even if blood glucose levels are less than 250 mg/dL. If ketoacidosis is suspected, discontinue canagliflozin; metformin, evaluate the patient, and institute prompt treatment. Treatment of ketoacidosis may require insulin, fluid and carbohydrate replacement. Monitor patients for resolution of ketoacidosis before restarting canagliflozin; metformin. Withhold canagliflozin; metformin, if possible, in temporary clinical situations that could predispose patients to ketoacidosis. For patients who undergo scheduled surgery, canagliflozin; metformin should be temporarily discontinued at least 3 days before surgery. Resume canagliflozin; metformin when the patient is clinically stable and has resumed oral intake. Educate patients on the signs and symptoms of ketoacidosis and instruct patients to discontinue this product and seek medical attention immediately if signs and symptoms occur.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name

    General Information
    In an ISMP safety report, metformin was noted as 1 of the 25 drugs having the strongest signals for rhabdomyolysis with 20 cases reported over 1 year to the FDA Adverse Event Reporting System (FAERS).

    Headline
    new primary malignancy
    General Information
    Canagliflozin may increase the risk for new primary malignancy, specifically renal cell cancer. In the CANVAS trial (mean duration of follow-up of 5.7 years), the incidence of renal cell carcinoma was 0.15% (2/1,331) and 0.29% (8/2,716) for placebo and canagliflozin, respectively. Patients with less than 6 months of follow-up, less than 90 days of treatment, or history of renal cell carcinoma were excluded. Due to the limited number of cases, a causal relationship to canagliflozin could not be established.
    Adverse Reaction GSTerms
      GSTerm Name
      new primary malignancy
      Severity
      Severe
    Headline
    laboratory test interference
    General Information
    Monitoring of glycemic control with urine glucose tests and the 1,5 Anhydroglucitol assay (1,5-AG assay) is not recommended in patients receiving canagliflozin; metformin due to laboratory test interference by canagliflozin. Use of urine glucose tests will result in positive urine glucose tests, and measurements of 1,5-AG are unreliable. Use alternative methods to monitor glycemic control.

Description
  Text
  Canagliflozin; metformin is a combination of 2 oral antidiabetic agents. Canagliflozin is a sodium-glucose co-transporter 2 (SGLT2) inhibitor. Metformin is a biguanide agent that improves insulin sensitivity. Canagliflozin; metformin combination products are indicated to improve glycemic control in adults and pediatric individuals aged 10 years and older with type 2 diabetes mellitus (T2DM). Based on clinical trials, when canagliflozin is added to metformin, the hemoglobin A1C is further reduced by a mean of 0.79% to 0.94%, which may assist T2DM individuals in achieving target A1C goals. Canagliflozin has been shown to reduce cardiovascular (CV) risk and heart failure (HF) risk in clinical trials. Canagliflozin significantly reduced the risk of major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE: CV death, the first occurrence of non-fatal myocardial infarction, or non-fatal stroke) by 14% relative to placebo (HR 0.86, 95% CI 0.75 to 0.97) in individuals T2DM and established CV disease. In the CREDENCE trial, the risk of the primary composite outcome of end-stage kidney disease (ESKD), doubling of serum creatinine, or death from renal or CV causes was 30% lower with canagliflozin treatment when compared with placebo. While metformin carries a low risk for lactic acidosis as outlined in the prescribing information and boxed warning of all metformin-containing products, care in prescribing and monitoring can reduce this risk. Results from these studies support canagliflozin monotherapy to reduce the risk of MACE in adults with T2DM and established CV disease and reduce ESKD, doubling of serum creatinine, CV death, and hospitalization for HF in adults with T2DM and diabetic nephropathy with albuminuria; however, the canagliflozin; metformin combination products are not approved for these indications. First-line T2DM therapy depends on comorbidities, individual-centered treatment factors, and management needs. In individuals with T2DM with or at high risk for atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD), heart failure (HF), and/or chronic kidney disease (CKD), certain glucagon-like peptide-1 receptor agonists (GLP-1 RAs) or SGLT2 inhibitors with proven benefit (e.g., canagliflozin) are appropriate initial therapy, with or without metformin based on glycemic needs. Canagliflozin; metformin combination products were initially FDA approved in 2014.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  Combination products containing metformin and canagliflozin are used to improve glycemic control in type 2 diabetes mellitus. Clinicians may wish to consult the individual monographs for more information about each agent.
Metformin: Metformin decreases hepatic gluconeogenesis production, decreases intestinal absorption of glucose, and improves insulin sensitivity by increasing peripheral glucose uptake and utilization; insulin secretion remains unchanged while fasting insulin levels and day-long plasma insulin response may actually decrease. Metformin improves glucose utilization in skeletal muscle and adipose tissue by increasing cell membrane glucose transport. This effect may be due to improved binding of insulin to insulin receptors since metformin is not effective in diabetics without some residual functioning pancreatic islet cells. Metformin causes a 10 to 20% decrease in fatty-acid oxidation and a slight increase in glucose oxidation. Unlike phenformin, metformin does not inhibit the mitochondrial oxidation of lactate unless its plasma concentrations become excessive (i.e., in patients with renal failure) and/or hypoxia is present. Clinically, metformin lowers fasting and postprandial hyperglycemia. The decrease in fasting plasma glucose is approximately 25 to 30%. Unlike oral sulfonylureas, it rarely causes hypoglycemia. Thus, metformin demonstrates more of an antihyperglycemic action than a hypoglycemic action. Metformin does not cause weight gain and in fact, may cause a modest weight loss due to drug-induced anorexia. Metformin also decreases plasma VLDL triglycerides resulting in modest decreases in plasma triglycerides and total cholesterol. Patients receiving metformin show a significant improvement in hemoglobin A1c, and a tendency toward improvement in the lipid profile, especially when baseline values are abnormally elevated.
Canagliflozin: Canagliflozin is an inhibitor of sodium-glucose co-transporter 2 (SGLT2), the transporter responsible for reabsorbing the majority of glucose filtered by the tubular lumen in the kidney. SGLT2 is expressed in the proximal renal tubules. By inhibiting SGLT2, canagliflozin reduces reabsorption of filtered glucose and lowers the renal threshold for glucose (RTG), and thereby increases urinary glucose excretion, improving blood glucose control.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Oral Route
    Description
    Metformin: The bioavailability of metformin is 50 to 60%. When the immediate-release combination tablets of canagliflozin; metformin (Invokamet) are administered with food, there is no change in metformin AUC; however, the mean peak plasma concentration of metformin was decreased by 16%. A delayed time to peak plasma concentration (1 hour) was observed under fed conditions. These changes are not likely to be clinically meaningful and therefore, it is recommended to take canagliflozin; metformin with meals. After administration of the extended-release combination tablets (Invokamet XR) with a high-fat breakfast, the AUC of metformin increased by approximately 61% and Cmax increased by approximately 13% relative to dosing in the fasted state; the extended-release tablets should be administered with the first main meal of the morning.
Canagliflozin: The mean absolute oral bioavailability of canagliflozin is approximately 65%. Following single-dose oral administration of canagliflozin 100 mg and 300 mg, peak plasma concentrations (median Tmax) occur within 1 to 2 hours post-dose. Plasma peak concentrations (Cmax) and the AUC of canagliflozin increase in a dose-proportional manner from 50 mg to 300 mg. Administration of the immediate-release combination tablets (Invokamet) with food resulted in no change in overall exposure of canagliflozin. A delayed time to peak plasma concentration (2 hours) was observed under fed conditions. After administration of extended-release (Invokamet XR) with a high-fat breakfast, the peak (Cmax) and total (AUC) exposure of canagliflozin were not altered relative to dosing in the fasted state. These changes are not likely to be clinically meaningful and therefore, it is recommended to take canagliflozin; metformin with meals.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    Specific pharmacokinetic studies have not been performed in patients with hepatic dysfunction receiving canagliflozin; metformin; however hepatic impairment increases the risk of lactic acidosis.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    Metformin: Metformin will accumulate in patients with moderate to severe renal dysfunction. Increasing renal dysfunction increases the risk of lactic acidosis from metformin. In patients with severe renal impairment with an eGFR below 30 mL/minute/1.73 m2, the steady-state exposure was approximately 40% higher than that of patients with normal renal function (AUC increase of 42% and Cmax increase of 35%). Metformin is removed by hemodialysis. The combination product of canagliflozin; metformin is contraindicated in patients with an eGFR below 30 mL/minute/1.73 m2.
Canagliflozin: Plasma protein binding is not meaningfully altered in patients with renal impairment. Renal impairment did not affect the Cmax of canagliflozin 200 mg. When the plasma AUC of canagliflozin was compared between healthy subjects and patients with various degrees of renal impairment, the plasma AUC increased with increasing severity of renal impairment; however, the plasma AUC of canagliflozin was similar for patients with end-stage renal disease (ESRD) and healthy adult subjects. The results were not considered clinically relevant. The pharmacodynamic response to canagliflozin declines with increasing severity of renal impairment, and thus, use is contraindicated in severe renal impairment (eGFR below 30 mL/minute/1.73 m2). Canagliflozin is negligibly removed by hemodialysis.
    Name
    Pediatrics
    Description
    Pharmacokinetic and pharmacodynamic studies of canagliflozin; metformin in pediatric patients aged 10 years and older resulted in exposures and responses consistent with those found in adult patients.
    Name
    Geriatric
    Description
    Renal function abnormalities can occur after initiating canagliflozin; metformin. Because both canagliflozin and metformin are substantially excreted by the kidney, and aging can be associated with reduced renal function, monitor renal function more frequently after initiating canagliflozin; metformin in the elderly and then adjust dose based on renal function. Age had no clinically meaningful effect on the pharmacokinetics of canagliflozin based on a population pharmacokinetic analysis.
    Name
    Gender Differences
    Description
    Gender did not significantly affect the pharmacokinetics of canagliflozin; metformin.
    Name
    Ethnic Differences
    Description
    Race did not significantly affect the pharmacokinetics of canagliflozin; metformin.
    Name
    Obesity
    Description
    Weight (BMI) did not significantly affect the pharmacokinetics of canagliflozin; metformin.
  Text
  Canagliflozin; metformin is administered orally. Bioequivalence between oral administration of the separate components and the combination products has been demonstrated.
Metformin: Metformin is distributed rapidly into peripheral body tissues and fluids and appears to distribute slowly into erythrocytes and to a deep tissue compartment (most likely GI tissues). The highest concentrations of metformin are found in the GI tract (10 times the concentrations in the plasma) and lower concentrations in the kidney, liver, and salivary gland tissue. Metformin is negligibly bound to plasma proteins. The average apparent volume of distribution (V/F) of metformin imediate-release tablets following a single, 850 mg dose is 654 +/- 358 L. Steady-state concentrations of metformin are reached within 1 to 2 days and are generally less than 1 mcg/mL. Metformin is not metabolized by the liver and this fact may explain why the risk of lactic acidosis is much less for metformin than for phenformin (i.e., approximately 10% of patients have an inherited defect in the ability to metabolize phenformin). The drug is excreted by the kidneys, largely unchanged, through an active tubular process; tubular secretion may be altered by many cationic drugs. Approximately 10% of an oral dose is excreted in the feces, presumably as unabsorbed metformin, and about 90% of a dose is excreted by the kidneys within 24 hours. Biliary excretion does not occur. Although the average elimination half-life in the plasma is approximately 6.2 hours in patients with normal renal function, metformin accumulates in red blood cells, which leads to a much longer elimination half-life in the blood (17.6 hours).
Canagliflozin: Canagliflozin is well distributed into tissues; in healthy subjects after a single IV infusion, the mean steady-state volume of distribution was 83.5 L. It is 99% bound plasma proteins, mainly to albumin. Protein binding is independent of plasma concentrations. Canagliflozin is mainly metabolized by O-glucuronidation via UGT1A9 and UGT2B4 to 2 inactive O-glucuronide metabolites. CYP3A4-mediated (oxidative) metabolism is approximately 7% in humans. Following administration of a single oral radioactive dose, 41.5%, 7%, and 3.2% of the administered dose was recovered in feces as canagliflozin, a hydroxylated metabolite, and an O-glucuronide metabolite, respectively. Enterohepatic circulation of canagliflozin was negligible. Approximately 33% of the administered radioactive dose was excreted in urine, mainly as O-glucuronide metabolites (30.5%). Less than 1% of the dose was excreted as unchanged canagliflozin in urine. Renal clearance of 100 mg and 300 mg PO doses ranged from 1.30 to 1.55 mL/min. Mean systemic clearance was approximately 192 mL/min following IV administration in healthy subjects.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 (CYP450) isoenzymes and drug transporters: Organic cationic transporter-2 (OCT2), multidrug and toxin extrusion (MATE1 and MATE2k), UGT1A9, UGT2B4, P-glycoprotein (P-gp)
Metformin: Drugs that interfere with common renal tubular transport systems involved in the renal elimination of metformin (e.g., organic cationic transporter-2 [OCT2]/multidrug and toxin extrusion [MATE1 and MATE2k] inhibitors such as ranolazine, vandetanib, dolutegravir, and cimetidine) could increase systemic exposure to metformin. An interaction between metformin and oral cimetidine has been observed, with a 60% increase in peak metformin plasma and whole blood concentrations and a 40% increase in plasma and whole blood metformin AUC, but no change in metformin elimination half-life. Careful patient monitoring and dose adjustment of metformin and/or the potentially interfering drug is recommended with concurrent use.
Canagliflozin: Canagliflozin is mainly metabolized via O-glucuronidation by a UGT1A9 and UGT2B4. CYP3A4-mediated metabolism is minimal in humans. Only inducers of these UGT subtypes are expected to have a significant effect on canagliflozin exposure. If a UGT inducer is coadministered with canagliflozin, increase the total daily dose of canagliflozin to 200 mg in patients tolerating a total daily dose of canagliflozin 100 mg. The total daily dose of canagliflozin may be increased to a maximum of 300 mg in patients currently tolerating canagliflozin 200 mg. Canagliflozin is a weak inhibitor of P-glycoprotein (P-gp) and is a substrate of drug transporters P-gp and MRP2; however, only a potential for pharmacokinetic interaction with digoxin has been considered relevant due to these effects. Canagliflozin did not induce CYP450 enzyme expression (CYP3A4, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2B6, and CYP1A2) in cultured human hepatocytes. It did not inhibit the CYP450 isoenzymes (CYP1A2, CYP2A6, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, or CYP2E1) and weakly inhibited CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, and CYP3A4 based on in vitro studies with human hepatic microsomes. Clinically relevant interactions with drugs affected by these CYP enzymes have not been reported.

Administration
  General Information
  For storage information, see the specific product information within the How Supplied section.
  Route Specific Administrations
    Administration Details
      Name
      Oral Solid Formulations
      Description
      General Dosing Information
Missed Dose: If a dose is missed, instruct patients to take the dose as soon as possible. Advise patients not to double up the next dose.
 
Immediate-release tablets
Administer the tablet twice daily with food.
 
Extended-release tablets
Administer tablets once daily with the morning meal. Swallow whole. Do not cut, crush, or chew.
The inactive ingredients may occasionally be eliminated in the feces as a soft mass that may resemble the original tablet.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Avoid use in patients with clinical or laboratory evidence of hepatic disease as there is an increased risk of lactic acidosis secondary to the use of metformin.
  Renal Impairment
  eGFR more than 60 mL/minute/1.73 m2: No dosage adjustment needed. Obtain an eGFR at least annually in all patients taking canagliflozin; metformin.
eGFR 45 to 59 mL/minute/1.73 m2: No metformin dosage adjustment needed. Do not exceed canagliflozin 100 mg/day (two 50 mg tablets) PO.
eGFR 30 to less than 45 mL/minute/1.73 m2: Assess the benefit/risk of continuing canagliflozin; metformin. Do not exceed canagliflozin 100 mg/day (two 50 mg tablets) PO.
eGFR below 30 mL/minute/1.73 m2: Use is contraindicated.
 
Intermittent hemodialysis
Metformin is dialyzable; hemodialysis will efficiently remove accumulated metformin in the case of drug-induced lactic acidosis, provided metformin is halted.
